People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I know it may sound excessive, but it feels hard to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety settles down. It’s been taking hours sometimes, and I thought maybe I should ask for help with it.

I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I know logically I’ve usually done it properly. But even after I’ve cleaned a wound area, or reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in like, “What if I missed one tiny thing?”\n\nAnd then it feels like that tiny thing could turn into something serious, like an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I can tell myself it’s probably fine, but it doesn’t really… settle just because I reason with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checking rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a specific way, though I know it probably sounds a bit silly.\n\nIt’s like I have to go through the same sequence with things—checking the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—and if I do it out of order or I don’t feel certain, I end up starting again. I don’t really feel… allowed to move on until I’ve done it enough times that the anxiety comes down. Sometimes that ends up taking hours, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do, beyond these checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… not really anything separate that I can think of. It’s mostly the rechecking.\n\nI mean, that’s kind of what’s bringing me in — I lose a lot of time going back over the same things: my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not that I want to be difficult or anything, it just feels like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSo… I guess the “habit” is mainly that repeated checking routine. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or just right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of things needing to be even or lined up perfectly.\n\nIt’s more about whether I feel sure I checked it properly. Like, if something looks a little off, I might recheck it, but I don’t think it’s because it isn’t symmetrical or “just right.” It’s more that I’m worried I might have missed something. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals have anything to do with making sure things are in order or arranged in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, I don’t think… like, I’m not trying to make things neat or in a certain order for its own sake.\n\nIt’s more that I have this routine I feel like I have to go through — with the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — and if I don’t feel certain, I can’t really move on. I keep rechecking until the anxiety comes down. It can take hours sometimes, which… I know isn’t great.\n\nSo I guess it’s less about things being arranged perfectly, and more about needing to be sure I checked them the right way. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I think it’s… more about avoiding mistakes, but also getting that feeling that things are under control, if that makes sense.

Like, I’ll tell myself I’m just being careful — checking the dressing supplies, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes — but then I can lose hours because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet. I have to go through the same routine enough times until the anxiety drops.

I know that probably sounds excessive… but in the moment it feels like if I stop too soon, I might have missed something important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I’m here because I’ve been spending a lot of time rechecking things. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s things like my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes… I kind of get stuck going through the same routine again and again. I know it may sound excessive, but it feels hard to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety settles down. It’s been taking hours sometimes, and I thought maybe I should ask for help with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I know logically I’ve usually done it properly. But even after I’ve cleaned a wound area, or reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in like, “What if I missed one tiny thing?”\n\nAnd then it feels like that tiny thing could turn into something serious, like an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I can tell myself it’s probably fine, but it doesn’t really… settle just because I reason with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checking rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a specific way, though I know it probably sounds a bit silly.\n\nIt’s like I have to go through the same sequence with things—checking the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—and if I do it out of order or I don’t feel certain, I end up starting again. I don’t really feel… allowed to move on until I’ve done it enough times that the anxiety comes down. Sometimes that ends up taking hours, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do, beyond these checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… not really anything separate that I can think of. It’s mostly the rechecking.\n\nI mean, that’s kind of what’s bringing me in — I lose a lot of time going back over the same things: my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not that I want to be difficult or anything, it just feels like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSo… I guess the “habit” is mainly that repeated checking routine. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or just right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of things needing to be even or lined up perfectly.\n\nIt’s more about whether I feel sure I checked it properly. Like, if something looks a little off, I might recheck it, but I don’t think it’s because it isn’t symmetrical or “just right.” It’s more that I’m worried I might have missed something. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals have anything to do with making sure things are in order or arranged in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, I don’t think… like, I’m not trying to make things neat or in a certain order for its own sake.\n\nIt’s more that I have this routine I feel like I have to go through — with the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — and if I don’t feel certain, I can’t really move on. I keep rechecking until the anxiety comes down. It can take hours sometimes, which… I know isn’t great.\n\nSo I guess it’s less about things being arranged perfectly, and more about needing to be sure I checked them the right way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about contamination or germs when you're rechecking your dressing supplies?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not germs in general all day or anything, but with the dressing supplies it does come up.\n\nEven after I’ve cleaned the wound area, there’s this thought that cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny thing and it turns into an infection? And I know I’ve already done it, logically, but it doesn’t really settle just because I tell myself that.\n\nSo I end up checking again, just to feel sure. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by contamination, but… that’s the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals help you avoid making mistakes, or is it more about feeling in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about avoiding mistakes, but also getting that feeling that things are under control, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just being careful — checking the dressing supplies, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes — but then I can lose hours because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet. I think it’s more the certainty part… like needing to feel I’ve done it correctly before I can let it go.

I do want to feel in control, I guess, but what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things — dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.

I know that sounds a bit much when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I’m just trying to be sure. Is that… is that the distinction you’re asking about?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I’m here because I’ve been spending a lot of time rechecking things. I know it may sound excessive, but it feels hard to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety settles down. It’s been taking hours sometimes, and I thought maybe I should ask for help with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I know logically I’ve usually done it properly. But even after I’ve cleaned a wound area, or reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in like, “What if I missed one tiny thing?”\n\nAnd then it feels like that tiny thing could turn into something serious, like an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I can tell myself it’s probably fine, but it doesn’t really… settle just because I reason with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checking rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a specific way, though I know it probably sounds a bit silly.\n\nIt’s like I have to go through the same sequence with things—checking the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—and if I do it out of order or I don’t feel certain, I end up starting again. Sometimes that ends up taking hours, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do, beyond these checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… not really anything separate that I can think of. It’s mostly the rechecking.\n\nI mean, that’s kind of what’s bringing me in — I lose a lot of time going back over the same things: my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals have anything to do with making sure things are in order or arranged in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, I don’t think… like, I’m not trying to make things neat or in a certain order for its own sake.\n\nIt’s more that I have this routine I feel like I have to go through — with the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — and if I don’t feel certain, I can’t really move on. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about contamination or germs when you're rechecking your dressing supplies?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not germs in general all day or anything, but with the dressing supplies it does come up.\n\nEven after I’ve cleaned the wound area, there’s this thought that cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny thing and it turns into an infection? I think even then, I’d still feel this pull to check, because it’s not only the actual harm. It’s that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety comes down. With the dressing supplies, or the door lock, or even work stuff like file dimensions and revision notes, I can know it’s probably fine, but it still doesn’t feel finished.

I know that may sound a little irrational… I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just that the checking is what makes it feel settled, even if only for a while.

Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals have anything to do with making sure things are in order or arranged in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, I don’t think… like, I’m not trying to make things neat or in a certain order for its own sake.\n\nIt’s more that I have this routine I feel like I have to go through — with the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — and if I don’t feel certain, I can’t really move on. I think… probably more about preventing mistakes than making everything perfect.

I mean, I don’t need it to be perfect in some ideal way, exactly. It’s more that I worry I’ve missed something, so I keep rechecking — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — until it feels like I’m allowed to move on.

Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or just right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of things needing to be even or lined up perfectly.\n\nIt’s more about whether I feel sure I checked it properly. Like, if something looks a little off, I might recheck it, but I don’t think it’s because it isn’t symmetrical or “just right.” It’s more that I’m worried I might have missed something. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals have anything to do with making sure things are in order or arranged in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, I don’t think… like, I’m not trying to make things neat or in a certain order for its own sake.\n\nIt’s more that I have this routine I feel like I have to go through — with the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — and if I don’t feel certain, I can’t really move on. I keep rechecking until the anxiety comes down. It can take hours sometimes, which… I know isn’t great.\n\nSo I guess it’s less about things being arranged perfectly, and more about needing to be sure I checked them the right way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about contamination or germs when you're rechecking your dressing supplies?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not germs in general all day or anything, but with the dressing supplies it does come up.\n\nEven after I’ve cleaned the wound area, there’s this thought that cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny thing and it turns into an infection? And I know I’ve already done it, logically, but it doesn’t really settle just because I tell myself that.\n\nSo I end up checking again, just to feel sure. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by contamination, but… that’s the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals help you avoid making mistakes, or is it more about feeling in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about avoiding mistakes, but also getting that feeling that things are under control, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just being careful — checking the dressing supplies, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes — but then I can lose hours because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet. I have to go through the same routine enough times until the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive… but in the moment it feels like if I stop too soon, I might have missed something important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to feel certain that you've done something correctly, or to feel like you're in control of what might happen if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the certainty part… like needing to feel I’ve done it correctly before I can let it go.\n\nI do want to feel in control, I guess, but what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things — dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that sounds a bit much when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I’m just trying to be sure. Is that… is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things repeatedly if you were absolutely certain that no harm would come from not checking?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that I could just stop if I knew nothing bad would happen… but I’m not totally sure.\n\nI think even then, I’d still feel this pull to check, because it’s not only the actual harm. It’s that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety comes down. With the dressing supplies, or the door lock, or even work stuff like file dimensions and revision notes, I can know it’s probably fine, but it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational… I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just that the checking is what makes it feel settled, even if only for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about preventing mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… probably more about preventing mistakes than making everything perfect.\n\nI mean, I don’t need it to be perfect in some ideal way, exactly. It’s more that I worry I’ve missed something, so I keep rechecking — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — until it feels like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, because I know it’s more than just being careful. I can lose hours to it sometimes, just going through the same routine until the anxiety drops. Does that answer it okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Maybe somewhat, yeah… if I could really accept that small mistakes happen, I think it might take some pressure off.

But honestly, I don’t know if it would make the checking stop. What gets me is that feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by contamination, but… that’s the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals help you avoid making mistakes, or is it more about feeling in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about avoiding mistakes, but also getting that feeling that things are under control, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just being careful — checking the dressing supplies, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes — but then I can lose hours because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet. I have to go through the same routine enough times until the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive… but in the moment it feels like if I stop too soon, I might have missed something important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to feel certain that you've done something correctly, or to feel like you're in control of what might happen if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the certainty part… like needing to feel I’ve done it correctly before I can let it go.\n\nI do want to feel in control, I guess, but what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things — dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that sounds a bit much when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I’m just trying to be sure. Is that… is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things repeatedly if you were absolutely certain that no harm would come from not checking?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that I could just stop if I knew nothing bad would happen… but I’m not totally sure.\n\nI think even then, I’d still feel this pull to check, because it’s not only the actual harm. Kind of, yes… but I wouldn’t say it’s about perfection exactly. It’s more that I have to do the checking in the same sort of routine until it feels “done,” if that makes sense.

I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by contamination, but… that’s the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals help you avoid making mistakes, or is it more about feeling in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about avoiding mistakes, but also getting that feeling that things are under control, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just being careful — checking the dressing supplies, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes — but then I can lose hours because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet. I have to go through the same routine enough times until the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive… but in the moment it feels like if I stop too soon, I might have missed something important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to feel certain that you've done something correctly, or to feel like you're in control of what might happen if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the certainty part… like needing to feel I’ve done it correctly before I can let it go.\n\nI do want to feel in control, I guess, but what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things — dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that sounds a bit much when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I’m just trying to be sure. It’s that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety comes down. It’s just that the checking is what makes it feel settled, even if only for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about preventing mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… probably more about preventing mistakes than making everything perfect.\n\nI mean, I don’t need it to be perfect in some ideal way, exactly. I can lose hours to it sometimes, just going through the same routine until the anxiety drops. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals have anything to do with making sure things are in order or arranged in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, I don’t think… like, I’m not trying to make things neat or in a certain order for its own sake.\n\nIt’s more that I have this routine I feel like I have to go through — with the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — and if I don’t feel certain, I can’t really move on. I keep rechecking until the anxiety comes down. It can take hours sometimes, which… I know isn’t great.\n\nSo I guess it’s less about things being arranged perfectly, and more about needing to be sure I checked them the right way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about contamination or germs when you're rechecking your dressing supplies?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not germs in general all day or anything, but with the dressing supplies it does come up.\n\nEven after I’ve cleaned the wound area, there’s this thought that cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny thing and it turns into an infection? And I know I’ve already done it, logically, but it doesn’t really settle just because I tell myself that.\n\nSo I end up checking again, just to feel sure. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by contamination, but… that’s the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals help you avoid making mistakes, or is it more about feeling in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about avoiding mistakes, but also getting that feeling that things are under control, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just being careful — checking the dressing supplies, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes — but then I can lose hours because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet. I have to go through the same routine enough times until the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive… but in the moment it feels like if I stop too soon, I might have missed something important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to feel certain that you've done something correctly, or to feel like you're in control of what might happen if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the certainty part… like needing to feel I’ve done it correctly before I can let it go.\n\nI do want to feel in control, I guess, but what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things — dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that sounds a bit much when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I’m just trying to be sure. Is that… is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things repeatedly if you were absolutely certain that no harm would come from not checking?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that I could just stop if I knew nothing bad would happen… but I’m not totally sure.\n\nI think even then, I’d still feel this pull to check, because it’s not only the actual harm. It’s that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety comes down. With the dressing supplies, or the door lock, or even work stuff like file dimensions and revision notes, I can know it’s probably fine, but it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational… I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just that the checking is what makes it feel settled, even if only for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about preventing mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… probably more about preventing mistakes than making everything perfect.\n\nI mean, I don’t need it to be perfect in some ideal way, exactly. It’s more that I worry I’ve missed something, so I keep rechecking — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — until it feels like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, because I know it’s more than just being careful. I can lose hours to it sometimes, just going through the same routine until the anxiety drops. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would decrease if you were able to accept that some small mistakes are inevitable?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, yeah… if I could really accept that small mistakes happen, I think it might take some pressure off.\n\nBut honestly, I don’t know if it would make the checking stop. What gets me is that feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet. I end up rechecking things — like dressing supplies, the door lock, file dimensions, revision notes — and it can take up hours because I’m waiting for the anxiety to come down enough.\n\nI know everyone makes small mistakes, and I can say that logically. It’s just… in the moment, it still feels like I need to go through the routine again before I can leave it alone. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be done in a very precise or exact way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… but I wouldn’t say it’s about perfection exactly. It’s more that I have to do the checking in the same sort of routine until it feels “done,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, or revision notes, I can get stuck going back over them because I don’t feel allowed to move on yet. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s things like my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes… I kind of get stuck going through the same routine again and again. I know it may sound excessive, but it feels hard to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety settles down. It’s been taking hours sometimes, and I thought maybe I should ask for help with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I know logically I’ve usually done it properly. But even after I’ve cleaned a wound area, or reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in like, “What if I missed one tiny thing?”\n\nAnd then it feels like that tiny thing could turn into something serious, like an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I can tell myself it’s probably fine, but it doesn’t really… settle just because I reason with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checking rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a specific way, though I know it probably sounds a bit silly.\n\nIt’s like I have to go through the same sequence with things—checking the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—and if I do it out of order or I don’t feel certain, I end up starting again. I don’t really feel… allowed to move on until I’ve done it enough times that the anxiety comes down. Sometimes that ends up taking hours, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do, beyond these checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… not really anything separate that I can think of. It’s mostly the rechecking.\n\nI mean, that’s kind of what’s bringing me in — I lose a lot of time going back over the same things: my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not that I want to be difficult or anything, it just feels like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSo… I guess the “habit” is mainly that repeated checking routine. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or just right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of things needing to be even or lined up perfectly.\n\nIt’s more about whether I feel sure I checked it properly. Like, if something looks a little off, I might recheck it, but I don’t think it’s because it isn’t symmetrical or “just right.” It’s more that I’m worried I might have missed something. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals have anything to do with making sure things are in order or arranged in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, I don’t think… like, I’m not trying to make things neat or in a certain order for its own sake.\n\nIt’s more that I have this routine I feel like I have to go through — with the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — and if I don’t feel certain, I can’t really move on. I keep rechecking until the anxiety comes down. It can take hours sometimes, which… I know isn’t great.\n\nSo I guess it’s less about things being arranged perfectly, and more about needing to be sure I checked them the right way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about contamination or germs when you're rechecking your dressing supplies?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not germs in general all day or anything, but with the dressing supplies it does come up.\n\nEven after I’ve cleaned the wound area, there’s this thought that cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny thing and it turns into an infection? And I know I’ve already done it, logically, but it doesn’t really settle just because I tell myself that.\n\nSo I end up checking again, just to feel sure. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by contamination, but… that’s the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals help you avoid making mistakes, or is it more about feeling in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about avoiding mistakes, but also getting that feeling that things are under control, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just being careful — checking the dressing supplies, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes — but then I can lose hours because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet. I have to go through the same routine enough times until the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive… but in the moment it feels like if I stop too soon, I might have missed something important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to feel certain that you've done something correctly, or to feel like you're in control of what might happen if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the certainty part… like needing to feel I’ve done it correctly before I can let it go.\n\nI do want to feel in control, I guess, but what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things — dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that sounds a bit much when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I’m just trying to be sure. Is that… is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things repeatedly if you were absolutely certain that no harm would come from not checking?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that I could just stop if I knew nothing bad would happen… but I’m not totally sure.\n\nI think even then, I’d still feel this pull to check, because it’s not only the actual harm. It’s that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety comes down. With the dressing supplies, or the door lock, or even work stuff like file dimensions and revision notes, I can know it’s probably fine, but it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational… I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just that the checking is what makes it feel settled, even if only for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about preventing mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… probably more about preventing mistakes than making everything perfect.\n\nI mean, I don’t need it to be perfect in some ideal way, exactly. It’s more that I worry I’ve missed something, so I keep rechecking — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — until it feels like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, because I know it’s more than just being careful. I can lose hours to it sometimes, just going through the same routine until the anxiety drops. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would decrease if you were able to accept that some small mistakes are inevitable?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, yeah… if I could really accept that small mistakes happen, I think it might take some pressure off.\n\nBut honestly, I don’t know if it would make the checking stop. What gets me is that feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet. I end up rechecking things — like dressing supplies, the door lock, file dimensions, revision notes — and it can take up hours because I’m waiting for the anxiety to come down enough.\n\nI know everyone makes small mistakes, and I can say that logically. It’s just… in the moment, it still feels like I need to go through the routine again before I can leave it alone. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be done in a very precise or exact way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… but I wouldn’t say it’s about perfection exactly. It’s more that I have to do the checking in the same sort of routine until it feels “done,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, or revision notes, I can get stuck going back over them because I don’t feel allowed to move on yet. I know that probably sounds excessive, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but sometimes I do lose hours to it. It’s like I’m waiting for the anxiety to drop enough before I can stop.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean by precise or exact?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would stop if you were certain that everything was in its perfect place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would fully stop, no. Maybe it would help a little if I could see that everything was in the right place, but the problem is more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked it in the same routine enough times.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, or revision notes — I’ll know I’ve checked them, but I still get this anxiety that I missed something. So I keep going back over it until the feeling drops. I’m not proud of it, but that’s really what’s bringing me in… I’m losing hours to rechecking.\n\nDoes that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to check things repeatedly if everything was arranged exactly as you like it?\n\nPatient: I think… probably yes, honestly. Even if everything was arranged exactly the way I like it, I don’t think that would completely stop the urge.\n\nIt’s not only about the arrangement. What brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking things — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot, and I’m not trying to overstate it, but that’s the part that feels hard to control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

What brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking things — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot, and I’m not trying to overstate it, but that’s the part that feels hard to control.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your main concern when checking things that you might make a mistake, or that something will go wrong if you don't check?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / engineer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"Even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area or reviewed a CAD file, a thought keeps cutting in that I missed one tiny thing and it’s going to turn into an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone, and I can’t just reason it away.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it’s more that I’m worried something will go wrong if I don’t check, or that I’ve made some tiny mistake.

Like, even after I know I cleaned the wound area, or I’ve reviewed a CAD file, there’s still this thought that cuts in — like, what if I missed one small thing and it turns into an infection, or a design mistake that could hurt someone. I can tell myself I already checked it, and I know that logically, but it doesn’t really make the thought go away.

I don’t want to sound dramatic, but that’s usually what’s behind it. It’s not just wanting it to look right. It’s more like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I’m here because I’ve been spending a lot of time rechecking things. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s things like my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes… I kind of get stuck going through the same routine again and again. I know it may sound excessive, but it feels hard to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety settles down. It’s been taking hours sometimes, and I thought maybe I should ask for help with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I know logically I’ve usually done it properly. But even after I’ve cleaned a wound area, or reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in like, “What if I missed one tiny thing?”\n\nAnd then it feels like that tiny thing could turn into something serious, like an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I can tell myself it’s probably fine, but it doesn’t really… settle just because I reason with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checking rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a specific way, though I know it probably sounds a bit silly.\n\nIt’s like I have to go through the same sequence with things—checking the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—and if I do it out of order or I don’t feel certain, I end up starting again. I don’t really feel… allowed to move on until I’ve done it enough times that the anxiety comes down. Sometimes that ends up taking hours, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do, beyond these checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… not really anything separate that I can think of. It’s mostly the rechecking.\n\nI mean, that’s kind of what’s bringing me in — I lose a lot of time going back over the same things: my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not that I want to be difficult or anything, it just feels like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSo… I guess the “habit” is mainly that repeated checking routine. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or just right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of things needing to be even or lined up perfectly.\n\nIt’s more about whether I feel sure I checked it properly. Like, if something looks a little off, I might recheck it, but I don’t think it’s because it isn’t symmetrical or “just right.” It’s more that I’m worried I might have missed something. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals have anything to do with making sure things are in order or arranged in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, I don’t think… like, I’m not trying to make things neat or in a certain order for its own sake.\n\nIt’s more that I have this routine I feel like I have to go through — with the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — and if I don’t feel certain, I can’t really move on. I keep rechecking until the anxiety comes down. It can take hours sometimes, which… I know isn’t great.\n\nSo I guess it’s less about things being arranged perfectly, and more about needing to be sure I checked them the right way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about contamination or germs when you're rechecking your dressing supplies?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not germs in general all day or anything, but with the dressing supplies it does come up.\n\nEven after I’ve cleaned the wound area, there’s this thought that cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny thing and it turns into an infection? And I know I’ve already done it, logically, but it doesn’t really settle just because I tell myself that.\n\nSo I end up checking again, just to feel sure. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by contamination, but… that’s the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals help you avoid making mistakes, or is it more about feeling in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about avoiding mistakes, but also getting that feeling that things are under control, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just being careful — checking the dressing supplies, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes — but then I can lose hours because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet. I have to go through the same routine enough times until the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive… but in the moment it feels like if I stop too soon, I might have missed something important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to feel certain that you've done something correctly, or to feel like you're in control of what might happen if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the certainty part… like needing to feel I’ve done it correctly before I can let it go.\n\nI do want to feel in control, I guess, but what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things — dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that sounds a bit much when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I’m just trying to be sure. Is that… is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things repeatedly if you were absolutely certain that no harm would come from not checking?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that I could just stop if I knew nothing bad would happen… but I’m not totally sure.\n\nI think even then, I’d still feel this pull to check, because it’s not only the actual harm. It’s that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety comes down. With the dressing supplies, or the door lock, or even work stuff like file dimensions and revision notes, I can know it’s probably fine, but it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational… I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just that the checking is what makes it feel settled, even if only for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about preventing mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… probably more about preventing mistakes than making everything perfect.\n\nI mean, I don’t need it to be perfect in some ideal way, exactly. It’s more that I worry I’ve missed something, so I keep rechecking — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — until it feels like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, because I know it’s more than just being careful. I can lose hours to it sometimes, just going through the same routine until the anxiety drops. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would decrease if you were able to accept that some small mistakes are inevitable?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, yeah… if I could really accept that small mistakes happen, I think it might take some pressure off.\n\nBut honestly, I don’t know if it would make the checking stop. What gets me is that feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet. I end up rechecking things — like dressing supplies, the door lock, file dimensions, revision notes — and it can take up hours because I’m waiting for the anxiety to come down enough.\n\nI know everyone makes small mistakes, and I can say that logically. It’s just… in the moment, it still feels like I need to go through the routine again before I can leave it alone. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be done in a very precise or exact way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… but I wouldn’t say it’s about perfection exactly. It’s like I’m waiting for the anxiety to drop enough before I can stop.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean by precise or exact?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would stop if you were certain that everything was in its perfect place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would fully stop, no. Maybe it would help a little if I could see that everything was in the right place, but the problem is more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked it in the same routine enough times.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, or revision notes — I’ll know I’ve checked them, but I still get this anxiety that I missed something. ========== INPUT [patient] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more the mistake part… though I guess feeling in control is mixed in there too.

It’s like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area or reviewed a CAD file properly, this thought keeps cutting in that I missed one tiny thing. And then my mind goes to, what if that turns into an infection, or what if it’s a design mistake that could hurt someone.

I know that probably sounds excessive, and I can see logically that I already checked, but I can’t always just reason it away. Does that make sense?

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I’m here because I’ve been spending a lot of time rechecking things. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s things like my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes… I kind of get stuck going through the same routine again and again. I know it may sound excessive, but it feels hard to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety settles down. It’s been taking hours sometimes, and I thought maybe I should ask for help with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you check these things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I know logically I’ve usually done it properly. But even after I’ve cleaned a wound area, or reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in like, “What if I missed one tiny thing?”\n\nAnd then it feels like that tiny thing could turn into something serious, like an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I can tell myself it’s probably fine, but it doesn’t really… settle just because I reason with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these checking rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a specific way, though I know it probably sounds a bit silly.\n\nIt’s like I have to go through the same sequence with things—checking the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—and if I do it out of order or I don’t feel certain, I end up starting again. I don’t really feel… allowed to move on until I’ve done it enough times that the anxiety comes down. Sometimes that ends up taking hours, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do, beyond these checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… not really anything separate that I can think of. It’s mostly the rechecking.\n\nI mean, that’s kind of what’s bringing me in — I lose a lot of time going back over the same things: my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not that I want to be difficult or anything, it just feels like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSo… I guess the “habit” is mainly that repeated checking routine. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or just right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of things needing to be even or lined up perfectly.\n\nIt’s more about whether I feel sure I checked it properly. Like, if something looks a little off, I might recheck it, but I don’t think it’s because it isn’t symmetrical or “just right.” It’s more that I’m worried I might have missed something. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals have anything to do with making sure things are in order or arranged in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Not really arranged, I don’t think… like, I’m not trying to make things neat or in a certain order for its own sake.\n\nIt’s more that I have this routine I feel like I have to go through — with the dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — and if I don’t feel certain, I can’t really move on. I keep rechecking until the anxiety comes down. It can take hours sometimes, which… I know isn’t great.\n\nSo I guess it’s less about things being arranged perfectly, and more about needing to be sure I checked them the right way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about contamination or germs when you're rechecking your dressing supplies?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not germs in general all day or anything, but with the dressing supplies it does come up.\n\nEven after I’ve cleaned the wound area, there’s this thought that cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny thing and it turns into an infection? And I know I’ve already done it, logically, but it doesn’t really settle just because I tell myself that.\n\nSo I end up checking again, just to feel sure. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by contamination, but… that’s the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals help you avoid making mistakes, or is it more about feeling in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about avoiding mistakes, but also getting that feeling that things are under control, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just being careful — checking the dressing supplies, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes — but then I can lose hours because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet. I have to go through the same routine enough times until the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive… but in the moment it feels like if I stop too soon, I might have missed something important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to feel certain that you've done something correctly, or to feel like you're in control of what might happen if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the certainty part… like needing to feel I’ve done it correctly before I can let it go.\n\nI do want to feel in control, I guess, but what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things — dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that sounds a bit much when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I’m just trying to be sure. Is that… is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things repeatedly if you were absolutely certain that no harm would come from not checking?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that I could just stop if I knew nothing bad would happen… but I’m not totally sure.\n\nI think even then, I’d still feel this pull to check, because it’s not only the actual harm. It’s that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety comes down. With the dressing supplies, or the door lock, or even work stuff like file dimensions and revision notes, I can know it’s probably fine, but it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational… I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just that the checking is what makes it feel settled, even if only for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about preventing mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… probably more about preventing mistakes than making everything perfect.\n\nI mean, I don’t need it to be perfect in some ideal way, exactly. It’s more that I worry I’ve missed something, so I keep rechecking — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — until it feels like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, because I know it’s more than just being careful. I can lose hours to it sometimes, just going through the same routine until the anxiety drops. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would decrease if you were able to accept that some small mistakes are inevitable?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, yeah… if I could really accept that small mistakes happen, I think it might take some pressure off.\n\nBut honestly, I don’t know if it would make the checking stop. What gets me is that feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet. I end up rechecking things — like dressing supplies, the door lock, file dimensions, revision notes — and it can take up hours because I’m waiting for the anxiety to come down enough.\n\nI know everyone makes small mistakes, and I can say that logically. It’s just… in the moment, it still feels like I need to go through the routine again before I can leave it alone. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be done in a very precise or exact way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… but I wouldn’t say it’s about perfection exactly. It’s more that I have to do the checking in the same sort of routine until it feels “done,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, or revision notes, I can get stuck going back over them because I don’t feel allowed to move on yet. I know that probably sounds excessive, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but sometimes I do lose hours to it. It’s like I’m waiting for the anxiety to drop enough before I can stop.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean by precise or exact?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would stop if you were certain that everything was in its perfect place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would fully stop, no. Maybe it would help a little if I could see that everything was in the right place, but the problem is more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked it in the same routine enough times.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, or revision notes — I’ll know I’ve checked them, but I still get this anxiety that I missed something. So I keep going back over it until the feeling drops. I’m not proud of it, but that’s really what’s bringing me in… I’m losing hours to rechecking.\n\nDoes that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to check things repeatedly if everything was arranged exactly as you like it?\n\nPatient: I think… probably yes, honestly. Even if everything was arranged exactly the way I like it, I don’t think that would completely stop the urge.\n\nIt’s not only about the arrangement. What brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking things — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot, and I’m not trying to overstate it, but that’s the part that feels hard to control.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when checking things that you might make a mistake, or that something will go wrong if you don't check?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that I’m worried something will go wrong if I don’t check, or that I’ve made some tiny mistake.\n\nLike, even after I know I cleaned the wound area, or I’ve reviewed a CAD file, there’s still this thought that cuts in — like, what if I missed one small thing and it turns into an infection, or a design mistake that could hurt someone. I can tell myself I already checked it, and I know that logically, but it doesn’t really make the thought go away.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic, but that’s usually what’s behind it. It’s not just wanting it to look right. It’s more like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things repeatedly because you're afraid of making a mistake, or because you need to feel in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the mistake part… though I guess feeling in control is mixed in there too.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area or reviewed a CAD file properly, this thought keeps cutting in that I missed one tiny thing. And then my mind goes to, what if that turns into an infection, or what if it’s a design mistake that could hurt someone.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive, and I can see logically that I already checked, but I can’t always just reason it away. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. With the dressing supplies, or the door lock, or even work stuff like file dimensions and revision notes, I can know it’s probably fine, but it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational… I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just that the checking is what makes it feel settled, even if only for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about preventing mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… probably more about preventing mistakes than making everything perfect.\n\nI mean, I don’t need it to be perfect in some ideal way, exactly. It’s more that I worry I’ve missed something, so I keep rechecking — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — until it feels like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, because I know it’s more than just being careful. I can lose hours to it sometimes, just going through the same routine until the anxiety drops. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would decrease if you were able to accept that some small mistakes are inevitable?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, yeah… if I could really accept that small mistakes happen, I think it might take some pressure off.\n\nBut honestly, I don’t know if it would make the checking stop. What gets me is that feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet. I end up rechecking things — like dressing supplies, the door lock, file dimensions, revision notes — and it can take up hours because I’m waiting for the anxiety to come down enough.\n\nI know everyone makes small mistakes, and I can say that logically. It’s just… in the moment, it still feels like I need to go through the routine again before I can leave it alone. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be done in a very precise or exact way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… but I wouldn’t say it’s about perfection exactly. It’s more that I have to do the checking in the same sort of routine until it feels “done,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, or revision notes, I can get stuck going back over them because I don’t feel allowed to move on yet. I know that probably sounds excessive, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but sometimes I do lose hours to it. It’s like I’m waiting for the anxiety to drop enough before I can stop.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean by precise or exact?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would stop if you were certain that everything was in its perfect place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would fully stop, no. Maybe it would help a little if I could see that everything was in the right place, but the problem is more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked it in the same routine enough times.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, or revision notes — I’ll know I’ve checked them, but I still get this anxiety that I missed something. So I keep going back over it until the feeling drops. I’m not proud of it, but that’s really what’s bringing me in… I’m losing hours to rechecking.\n\nDoes that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to check things repeatedly if everything was arranged exactly as you like it?\n\nPatient: I think… probably yes, honestly. Even if everything was arranged exactly the way I like it, I don’t think that would completely stop the urge.\n\nIt’s not only about the arrangement. What brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking things — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot, and I’m not trying to overstate it, but that’s the part that feels hard to control.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when checking things that you might make a mistake, or that something will go wrong if you don't check?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that I’m worried something will go wrong if I don’t check, or that I’ve made some tiny mistake.\n\nLike, even after I know I cleaned the wound area, or I’ve reviewed a CAD file, there’s still this thought that cuts in — like, what if I missed one small thing and it turns into an infection, or a design mistake that could hurt someone. I can tell myself I already checked it, and I know that logically, but it doesn’t really make the thought go away.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic, but that’s usually what’s behind it. It’s not just wanting it to look right. It’s more like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things repeatedly because you're afraid of making a mistake, or because you need to feel in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the mistake part… though I guess feeling in control is mixed in there too.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area or reviewed a CAD file properly, this thought keeps cutting in that I missed one tiny thing. And then my mind goes to, what if that turns into an infection, or what if it’s a design mistake that could hurt someone.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive, and I can see logically that I already checked, but I can’t always just reason it away. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to feel certain that you've done something correctly, or to feel like you're in control of what might happen if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the certainty part… like needing to feel I’ve done it correctly before I can let it go.\n\nI do want to feel in control, I guess, but what brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things — dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nI know that sounds a bit much when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I’m just trying to be sure. Is that… is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things repeatedly if you were absolutely certain that no harm would come from not checking?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that I could just stop if I knew nothing bad would happen… but I’m not totally sure.\n\nI think even then, I’d still feel this pull to check, because it’s not only the actual harm. It’s that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety comes down. With the dressing supplies, or the door lock, or even work stuff like file dimensions and revision notes, I can know it’s probably fine, but it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational… I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just that the checking is what makes it feel settled, even if only for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about preventing mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… probably more about preventing mistakes than making everything perfect.\n\nI mean, I don’t need it to be perfect in some ideal way, exactly. It’s more that I worry I’ve missed something, so I keep rechecking — my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes — until it feels like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, because I know it’s more than just being careful. I can lose hours to it sometimes, just going through the same routine until the anxiety drops. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking behaviors would decrease if you were able to accept that some small mistakes are inevitable?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, yeah… if I could really accept that small mistakes happen, I think it might take some pressure off.\n\nBut honestly, I don’t know if it would make the checking stop. What gets me is that feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet. Unless maybe I’m not understanding what you mean exactly?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your checking behaviors would decrease if you no longer felt the need for things to be symmetrical or in a specific order?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / engineer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things—my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Probably not, honestly… I don’t think symmetry or a specific order is really the driver for me.

It’s more that I get stuck rechecking things until I feel like I’m “allowed” to move on. Like my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes… I’ll go back over them more than I need to, and I can lose a lot of time that way. I guess I’m waiting for the anxiety to drop enough that it feels safe to stop.

I don’t know if I’m explaining that right, but it’s less about things looking or feeling perfectly arranged, and more about not trusting that I’m done.
